                            "json_block": "{\"language\": \"plaintext\", \"code\": \"BRAIN-INSPIRED CONTINUAL LEARNING RESEARCH \\u2014 CONVERSATION EXPORT\\n==================================================================\\n(Exported for use as project context in Claude Code)\\n\\nPROJECT GOAL\\n------------\\nBuild a small, CPU-trainable neural network architecture inspired by how the\\nhuman brain learns and remembers, to explore continual learning without\\ncatastrophic forgetting. Independent researcher, no large compute budget,\\ntargeting an incremental, testable, publishable-scale project (arXiv /\\nworkshop level), not a full \\\"solve AGI\\\" attempt.\\n\\nCORE INTUITIONS THE USER DEVELOPED IN THIS CONVERSATION\\n---------------------------------------------------------\\n1. Current LLMs are fundamentally next-token predictors: random weight\\n   initialization -> forward pass -> loss -> backprop -> gradient descent,\\n   repeated over huge datasets. Embeddings self-organize into semantic\\n   clusters as an emergent side effect of this process (not manually designed).\\n\\n2. Core distinction between AI training and human brains:\\n   - AI: train-then-freeze. Weights are static at inference time.\\n   - Brain: continuous, always-on synaptic plasticity. No train/inference split.\\n   - This split is the root cause of \\\"catastrophic forgetting\\\" in ANNs.\\n\\n3. The brain is NOT randomly initialized like a fresh neural net. It has\\n   structured, evolution-specified wiring before experience (genomic\\n   bottleneck, critical periods, retinal waves, rich-club organization\\n   present before birth). Learning refines this scaffold; it doesn't build\\n   from scratch.\\n\\n4. The brain almost certainly does NOT run literal backpropagation\\n   (weight transport problem, need for symmetric forward/backward weights,\\n   non-local global error signals). Leading biologically plausible\\n   alternatives: Hebbian learning / STDP, predictive coding, target\\n   propagation, feedback alignment, equilibrium propagation, dendritic\\n   error microcircuits. Whether the brain approximates gradient descent via\\n   these local mechanisms (NGRAD hypothesis) remains actively debated\\n   (2023-2026 research: prospective configuration, target-learning studies).\\n\\n5. Memory is NOT a single monolithic store. Two axes:\\n   HORIZONTAL: specialized regions/modules handle different content types\\n   (faces, language, motor skills, episodic events) somewhat independently\\n   -- confirmed by patient H.M. losing hippocampus-dependent recall while\\n   retaining motor skill learning and familiarity-based recognition.\\n   Engineering analogue: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.\\n\\n   VERTICAL: layered memory tiers --\\n     sensory/cache buffer (milliseconds) ->\\n     working memory (seconds, small capacity) ->\\n     consolidation (hippocampus-driven, sleep-dependent, decides what gets\\n       promoted, compresses raw experience into gist) ->\\n     long-term memory (durable, itself split into episodic/semantic/procedural)\\n\\n6. What determines what gets consolidated into durable long-term memory:\\n   (a) REPETITION / spaced retrieval (testing effect, strengthens via LTP)\\n   (b) EMOTIONAL SALIENCE / \\\"surprise\\\" (amygdala tags significant events;\\n       hippocampal consolidation is boosted for emotionally arousing events)\\n   Content with neither repetition nor emotional salience tends to fade\\n   (synaptic pruning / downscaling, interference from new learning).\\n\\n7. Recognition vs. Recall are separate systems, not one system at different\\n   speeds:\\n   - Recognition/familiarity: fast, automatic, unconscious, huge capacity\\n     (dedicated face-recognition pathway through limbic areas).\\n   - Recall: slow, effortful, two-stage (search candidates, then verify),\\n     hippocampus-dependent, reconstructs rather than just matches.\\n\\n8. Fixed brain capacity + continuous new input => an active policy is\\n   REQUIRED for what to compress, strengthen, or drop. This is not a flaw;\\n   it's a necessary consequence of finite storage + infinite incoming\\n   experience.\\n\\n9. External memory (diary, notes, indexed archives) is the human workaround\\n   for the brain's necessary forgetting -- structurally identical in\\n   function to RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) systems used in\\n   current AI: embed content, store in a vector DB, retrieve by similarity\\n   at query time, optionally write new facts back. Real robots described\\n   in the conversation (\\\"robot remembers what you told it\\\") use exactly\\n   this RAG pattern, not weight updates -- the underlying model does NOT\\n   change; only an external database changes.\\n\\n10. Reviewed related work directly:\\n    - Titans (Behrouz, Zhong, Mirrokni; Google Research, Jan 2025,\\n      arXiv:2501.00663): neural long-term memory module that updates its\\n      OWN weights at test time via a \\\"surprise\\\" metric (gradient of loss\\n      w.r.t. input = how unexpected the input is), with a learned decay\\n      (forget) mechanism. Three integration variants: Memory as Context\\n      (MAC), Memory as Gate (MAG), Memory as Layer (MAL). Combined with\\n      short-term memory (limited-window attention) and fixed persistent\\n      memory (task knowledge). Claimed to beat Transformers and modern\\n      linear recurrent models (Mamba, DeltaNet, etc.), scale to >2M token\\n      context, and outperform GPT-4 / RAG-augmented Llama3.1-8B on\\n      BABILong long-context reasoning with far fewer parameters.\\n\\n    - \\\"Titans Revisited\\\" (Di Nepi, Siciliano, Silvestri; Sapienza\\n      University of Rome, Oct 2025, arXiv:2510.09551): independent\\n      lightweight reimplementation + critical reproduction study. Key\\n      findings:\\n        * Original paper had unresolved ambiguities (chunking strategy,\\n          prediction source, MAC block depth, attention hyperparameters),\\n          hindering reproducibility.\\n        * Neural Memory component CONSISTENTLY improved results over\\n          attention-only baselines across MLM, time series forecasting,\\n          and recommendation tasks.\\n        * Full Titans (MAC) did NOT always beat established baselines\\n          (e.g., lost to BERT4Rec on MovieLens recommendation), largely\\n          due to the performance cost/degradation from chunking.\\n        * CRITICAL FINDING: when they froze the Transformer backbone and\\n          updated ONLY the neural memory weights at test time over many\\n          epochs, performance stayed flat/slowly degraded -- i.e.,\\n          \\\"memory updates alone are insufficient for meaningful test-time\\n          learning.\\\" They attribute this to a mismatch between the frozen\\n          backbone's key/value projections and how the memory evolves,\\n          and propose JOINT adaptation of memory + backbone as necessary\\n          future work.\\n\\n    ==> IDENTIFIED OPEN RESEARCH GAP (small-scale, testable, currently\\n        unresolved as of these two papers): does jointly fine-tuning a\\n        small portion of the backbone alongside the neural memory (rather\\n        than freezing the backbone entirely) restore meaningful test-time\\n        learning? No published answer found in either paper.\\n\\nPROPOSED ARCHITECTURE (staged, layer by layer)\\n------------------------------------------------\\nTerminology clarification (user-identified and confirmed as a genuine,\\nfield-recognized distinction):\\n  - \\\"Weights\\\" = slow parameters, updated gradually via backprop over many\\n    examples, never from a single exposure (standard deep learning sense).\\n  - \\\"Memory state / fast weights\\\" = separate parameters that CAN change\\n    drastically from one or a few exposures, decay over time if not\\n    reinforced, and are updated by a local, immediate, surprise-gated rule\\n    -- NOT standard backprop over a training set. Directly maps to the\\n    user's question: \\\"can it remember something after seeing it once, and\\n    then forget it after a few days if unused?\\\" Answer: yes, this is\\n    exactly what the fast-weights/memory-state layer with a decay term is\\n    for; it's a categorically different object from a standard weight.\\n\\nLayer 0 -- Input encoder (slow weights): standard small network (MLP or\\n  small transformer) converting raw input into vector representations.\\n  Trained conventionally via backprop. Analogous to early sensory cortex.\\n\\nLayer 1 -- Working memory / cache: short buffer holding only current input\\n  (and maybe last few interactions). Not \\\"learned\\\" in the weight sense;\\n  temporary, constantly overwritten. Analogous to sensory/working memory.\\n\\nLayer 2 -- Surprise / salience gate: computes a scalar signal each step\\n  from the model's own prediction error (loss or gradient magnitude) on\\n  the current input -- high surprise = high salience = gates whether\\n  Layer 3 gets updated at all. Directly implements the amygdala-style\\n  \\\"is this significant?\\\" tagging mechanism, mathematically approximated\\n  the same way Titans does it.\\n\\nLayer 3 -- Fast memory / neural memory module: separate small associative\\n  memory network whose OWN parameters update at test/run time via a rule\\n  of the form:\\n      Memory_t = (1 - decay) * Memory_(t-1) + surprise_gated_update\\n  This is where \\\"remember after one exposure, fade after N days/steps if\\n  unreinforced\\\" behavior lives. Decay rate is a tunable hyperparameter\\n  directly controlling how long an un-reinforced memory persists.\\n\\nLayer 4 -- Consolidation / compression step: run PERIODICALLY (not every\\n  step, analogous to sleep). Scans current fast-memory contents; items\\n  reinforced multiple times (repetition) OR with high cumulative salience\\n  get compressed and promoted to Layer 5. Two competing implementation\\n  choices worth testing head-to-head:\\n    (a) fold consolidated info into slow weights via periodic fine-tuning\\n        (closer to biological cortex), or\\n    (b) write a compressed summary into an external retrievable database\\n        (closer to RAG / \\\"diary with topic-wise index\\\").\\n\\nLayer 5 -- Long-term store: durable retention, via (a) or (b) above.\\n\\nLayer 6 -- Modular routing (add LAST, after 0-5 validated individually):\\n  split memory into multiple parallel specialized instances via a\\n  Mixture-of-Experts-style router, so unrelated knowledge doesn't compete\\n  for the same storage slots. Implements the \\\"horizontal\\\" specialization\\n  axis (different brain regions for different content types).\\n\\nRECOMMENDED BUILD ORDER (staged, validate each before adding next)\\n---------------------------------------------------------------------\\n1. Baseline: plain small model trained sequentially on Task A then Task B\\n   (synthetic classification tasks in the same input space, different\\n   class clusters) -- to concretely OBSERVE catastrophic forgetting\\n   (accuracy on Task A collapses after training on Task B).\\n2. Add ONE existing mitigation (simple replay buffer, or EWC-style\\n   elastic weight consolidation) -- confirm it measurably reduces\\n   forgetting vs. the baseline.\\n3. Add the surprise-gated fast-memory layer (Layer 2+3 above) -- test\\n   whether it beats plain replay/EWC on retention of Task A while still\\n   learning Task B.\\n4. If 1-3 succeed, add a second memory tier (fast + slow, Fast-Weights /\\n   Titans style) and test whether combining tiers compounds the benefit.\\n5. Add modular routing (Layer 6) LAST -- most architecturally complex,\\n   easiest to introduce instability.\\n\\nRationale for this order: isolate one new mechanism at a time so any\\nimprovement (or failure) can be attributed to a specific, identifiable\\ndesign choice, rather than confounding multiple untested ideas at once.\\nEach stage, even if the project stops early, is a legitimate small\\nreproducibility/ablation-style result on its own.\\n\\nPRACTICAL / COMPUTE CONTEXT\\n------------------------------\\n- Independent researcher, no large GPU budget assumed by default.\\n- Small models (thousands to low millions of parameters), small synthetic\\n  or toy datasets, CPU-feasible for the early stages; free GPU (Google\\n  Colab / Kaggle) recommended once models/sequences grow, since CPU-only\\n  becomes painfully slow (hours per run) well before results get\\n  interesting at larger scale.\\n- User's own prior working style: started this exploration first in a\\n  sandboxed cloud code environment using raw NumPy (no internet access\\n  for pip installs, no PyTorch available in that sandbox) to hand-build\\n  Layer 0-3 with explicit forward passes and manual gradients, specifically\\n  so every weight update is visible and nothing is hidden inside a\\n  framework.                         "text": "Exported above — feed that into Claude Code as project context, it summarizes everything: the reasoning we built up, the two papers, the architecture design, and the recommended build order.\n\nNow, the practical obstacles you'll actually hit building this yourself, in order of how soon you'll meet them:\n\n**1. Environment/dependency setup.** On your own laptop you'll need Python + a framework (PyTorch is the standard choice — easier autograd than hand-rolling NumPy gradients). `pip install torch` normally, no issue like the sandbox I just hit.\n\n**2. Debugging \"silent\" bugs in the fast-memory update rule.** This is the big one specific to your design. A decay term like `Memory = (1-decay)*Memory + surprise_gated_update` is very easy to get numerically wrong — pick decay too high and everything forgets instantly (memory is useless); too low and nothing ever forgets (defeats the point, and can even destabilize training as the memory value grows unbounded). Expect to spend real time just tuning this one scalar before anything else makes sense.\n\n**3. Distinguishing \"it learned nothing\" from \"it forgot everything\" from \"the surprise gate never fires.\"** With three new moving parts (encoder, gate, memory), when results are bad you won't immediately know which piece is broken. Solve this by logging each piece's output separately from the start (surprise score per input, memory norm over time, accuracy per task) — don't just watch final accuracy.\n\n**4. The gate itself needs a stable reference to compare \"surprising\" against.** Surprise is defined relative to what the model already expects — if Layer 0's encoder is still updating rapidly (early training), the surprise signal is noisy and unreliable, and Layer 3 will store garbage. You'll likely need to let Layer 0 stabilize somewhat before trusting the gate.\n\n**5. Catastrophic forgetting baseline itself needs care to demonstrate cleanly.** Too easy a task (well-separated clusters) and even the naive model won't forget much; too hard and even your improved version won't help. Expect one round of tuning the synthetic task's difficulty before it's a good testbed.\n\n**6. Reproducibility discipline.** Fix random seeds everywhere, log hyperparameters with every run — otherwise you won't be able to tell if an improvement is real or just a lucky seed, which is exactly the kind of ambiguity the Titans reproduction paper called out in the original work.\n\nSolve these roughly in this order as you build — 1 and 2 first, since nothing else is testable until those work.",

                        "text": "Yes — and this is a genuinely important piece you haven't explicitly named yet, though it underlies almost everything we've discussed. Survival relevance is arguably the *original* salience signal, older than emotion as we consciously experience it.\n\n## Why survival instinct is the deeper layer under \"emotional salience\"\n\nWhen we talked about the amygdala tagging things as \"significant,\" we described it as an emotion-detection system. But evolutionarily, that system didn't evolve to track feelings for their own sake — it evolved because **organisms that remembered survival-relevant events (predators, food sources, danger, safety, poison) reproduced more than those that didn't.** Emotion is the *mechanism*; survival is the *reason the mechanism exists at all.* Fear, disgust, and even strong positive emotions (like the reward of eating or mating) are the brain's internal proxy for \"this matters for staying alive and passing on genes\" — not an arbitrary tagging system.\n\nThis is why:\n- **Threat-related and food-related memories are disproportionately vivid and durable**, even from a single exposure — your brain didn't evolve a general-purpose \"remember interesting things\" system; it evolved a specific bias toward survival-relevant categories.\n- **The amygdala specifically sits next to, and heavily influences, the hippocampus** — this isn't incidental wiring. It's a direct evolutionary solution: \"when something threat/survival-relevant happens, forcibly boost how strongly it gets written into long-term memory,\" because a single missed threat can be fatal, so waiting for repetition (like normal learning does) isn't an option.\n\n## This actually answers something from earlier in our conversation\n\nRemember your point about not needing huge repetition for some things — a poem you might memorize almost verbatim, while a whole book gets compressed to a paragraph? Survival relevance is one of the reasons *some* single exposures get treated specially and skip the \"needs repetition\" rule entirely. A snake, a scream, a near-accident — these get one-shot, high-fidelity storage precisely because your brain's ancient prioritization system flags them as \"can't afford to average this over many exposures, must remember now.\"\n\n## The important nuance — it's not literal \"will I die\" calculation\n\nThe brain doesn't compute actual survival probability. It runs *fast heuristics* evolved to correlate with survival across millions of years of ancestral environments — which is why these heuristics **misfire constantly in modern life.** You feel intense fear seeing a spider (correlated with ancestral survival) but no equivalent instinctive dread about, say, a dangerous financial decision or a slow-moving health risk, even though the latter may be far more threatening to your actual survival today. The system is optimizing for an ancient statistical pattern, not real-time rational risk assessment.\n\n## Direct relevance to your architecture\n\nThis suggests your surprise/salience gate (Layer 2 in our design) could have **two separate components**, not just one generic \"surprise\" score:\n1. **Statistical surprise** — how unexpected is this input given what the model already knows (this is what Titans' gradient-magnitude approach captures)\n2. **Domain-relevance weighting** — a separate, possibly hand-specified or task-specific prior that says \"certain categories of content get an extra boost regardless of statistical surprise\" (analogous to survival-relevant categories getting privileged treatment regardless of how novel they are)\n\nThis is actually a real, somewhat underexplored idea in continual learning research — most systems (including Titans) only implement component 1. Component 2 — a domain-specific, hardcoded \"this category always matters more\" prior layered on top of learned surprise — is closer to what biological survival-instinct wiring actually gives the brain, and could be a genuine point of novelty for your project rather than something already published.",

                            "file_text": "\"\"\"\nPrototype: a small model with a SELF-UPDATING fast-memory layer.\n\nTwo kinds of parameters, on purpose (matches our earlier discussion):\n  - `weights`      -> normal nn.Parameters, trained slowly via backprop + optimizer.step()\n  - `memory state` -> a plain tensor (NOT a registered nn.Parameter), updated by\n                       its own local rule every step, with decay (\"forgets\" if unused).\n\nRun: pip install torch      then      python prototype.py\n\"\"\"\n\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\nimport numpy as np\n\ntorch.manual_seed(42)\nrng = np.random.default_rng(42)\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------\n# 1. Toy continual-learning task: Task A then Task B, same input space,\n#    different class clusters -> naive models forget Task A after Task B.\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------\nN_FEATURES = 10\nN_CLASSES = 3\n\ndef make_task(n_samples, centers, noise=0.5):\n    X, y = [], []\n    for c, center in enumerate(centers):\n        pts = center + rng.normal(0, noise, size=(n_samples, N_FEATURES))\n        X.append(pts); y.append(np.full(n_samples, c))\n    X = np.concatenate(X); y = np.concatenate(y)\n    idx = rng.permutation(len(X))\n    return torch.tensor(X[idx], dtype=torch.float32), torch.tensor(y[idx], dtype=torch.long)\n\ncenters_A = rng.normal(0, 3, size=(N_CLASSES, N_FEATURES))\ncenters_B = rng.normal(0, 3, size=(N_CLASSES, N_FEATURES))\n\nX_A, y_A = make_task(300, centers_A)\nX_B, y_B = make_task(300, centers_B)\nX_A_test, y_A_test = make_task(100, centers_A)\nX_B_test, y_B_test = make_task(100, centers_B)\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------\n# 2. The model: Encoder (slow weights) + Surprise Gate + Fast Memory\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------\nclass BrainInspiredNet(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, in_dim, hidden_dim, n_classes, decay=0.05, surprise_threshold=0.5):\n        super().__init__()\n\n        # --- Layer 0: encoder (SLOW WEIGHTS, trained by backprop as usual) ---\n        self.encoder = nn.Sequential(\n            nn.Linear(in_dim, hidden_dim),\n            nn.ReLU(),\n        )\n        self.classifier = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, n_classes)\n\n        # --- Layer 3: fast memory (NOT an nn.Parameter -> not touched by optimizer) ---\n        # A simple associative memory: one vector summarizing \"what's been surprising lately\"\n        self.memory = torch.zeros(hidden_dim)\n        self.decay = decay                      # how fast unused memory fades\n        self.surprise_threshold = surprise_threshold\n\n        # bookkeeping for inspection/debugging (obstacle #3 from our list: log everything)\n        self.last_surprise = 0.0\n        self.memory_norm_history = []\n        self.surprise_history = []\n\n    def forward(self, x, use_memory=True):\n        h = self.encoder(x)                     # (batch, hidden_dim)\n        if use_memory:\n            # inject fast memory as a simple additive bias (Layer 3 -> Layer 0 feedback)\n            h = h + self.memory.unsqueeze(0)\n        logits = self.classifier(h)\n        return logits, h\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def update_memory(self, h_batch, loss_per_sample):\n        \"\"\"\n        Surprise-gated fast memory update (Layer 2 + Layer 3).\n        loss_per_sample: per-example loss (our 'surprise' signal, higher = more surprising)\n        h_batch: encoder outputs for this batch (used as the thing to memorize)\n        \"\"\"\n        surprise = loss_per_sample.mean().item()\n        self.last_surprise = surprise\n        self.surprise_history.append(surprise)\n\n        # gate: only write to memory if this batch was surprising enough\n        if surprise > self.surprise_threshold:\n            # weight each sample's contribution by how surprising IT specifically was\n            weights = (loss_per_sample / (loss_per_sample.sum() + 1e-8)).unsqueeze(1)\n            update = (weights * h_batch.detach()).sum(dim=0)\n            self.memory = (1 - self.decay) * self.memory + self.decay * update\n        else:\n            # even if not updated, memory still slowly decays if unreinforced\n            self.memory = (1 - self.decay) * self.memory\n\n        self.memory_norm_history.append(self.memory.norm().item())\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------\n# 3. Training loop that trains on Task A, then Task B, sequentially\n#    -- with the fast-memory mechanism active throughout.\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------\ndef evaluate(model, X, y, use_memory=True):\n    model.eval()\n    with torch.no_grad():\n        logits, _ = model(X, use_memory=use_memory)\n        preds = logits.argmax(dim=1)\n        acc = (preds == y).float().mean().item()\n    model.train()\n    return acc\n\ndef train_on_task(model, optimizer, X, y, epochs=30, batch_size=32):\n    n = len(X)\n    for epoch in range(epochs):\n        idx = torch.randperm(n)\n        for i in range(0, n, batch_size):\n            batch_idx = idx[i:i+batch_size]\n            xb, yb = X[batch_idx], y[batch_idx]\n\n            optimizer.zero_grad()\n            logits, h = model(xb, use_memory=True)\n            per_sample_loss = F.cross_entropy(logits, yb, reduction=\"none\")\n            loss = per_sample_loss.mean()\n            loss.backward()\n            optimizer.step()                      # <- slow weights updated here (backprop)\n\n            model.update_memory(h, per_sample_loss.detach())  # <- fast memory updated here (local rule)\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    model = BrainInspiredNet(in_dim=N_FEATURES, hidden_dim=32, n_classes=N_CLASSES,\n                             decay=0.05, surprise_threshold=0.8)\n    optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.01)  # only touches slow weights\n\n    print(\"=== Training on Task A ===\")\n    train_on_task(model, optimizer, X_A, y_A, epochs=30)\n    acc_A_after_A = evaluate(model, X_A_test, y_A_test)\n    print(f\"Accuracy on Task A right after learning A: {acc_A_after_A:.3f}\")\n\n    print(\"\\n=== Training on Task B (Task A no longer seen) ===\")\n    train_on_task(model, optimizer, X_B, y_B, epochs=30)\n    acc_A_after_B = evaluate(model, X_A_test, y_A_test)\n    acc_B_after_B = evaluate(model, X_B_test, y_B_test)\n\n    print(f\"Accuracy on Task A AFTER learning B: {acc_A_after_B:.3f}  <- forgetting shows up here if it drops\")\n    print(f\"Accuracy on Task B after learning B: {acc_B_after_B:.3f}\")\n\n    print(f\"\\nFinal memory vector norm: {model.memory.norm().item():.4f}\")\n    print(f\"Surprise history (last 10 batches): {[round(s,3) for s in model.surprise_history[-10:]]}\")\n    print(f\"Memory norm history (last 10): {[round(m,3) for m in model.memory_norm_history[-10:]]}\")\n",

It's updated by its own hand-written rule in `update_memory()` — your fast memory.\n- **Surprise gate** = per-sample loss; if a batch's average loss exceeds `surprise_threshold`, memory gets written; otherwise it just decays.\n- **Decay** = every step, memory fades by 5% unless reinforced (`decay=0.05`) — this is your \"forgets in a few days if not revisited\" behavior, just compressed into training steps instead of days.\n\n**What to watch when it runs:**\n- `acc_A_after_A` vs `acc_A_after_B` — if the second is much lower, you've reproduced catastrophic forgetting, live.\n- `surprise_history` — should start high (everything is surprising when untrained) and drop as the model settles.\n- `memory_norm_history` — should not blow up to infinity or collapse to zero; if it does, `decay` or `surprise_threshold` needs retuning (obstacle #2 from our list, happening in real time).\n\nRun it, tell me the actual numbers you get, and we'll diagnose together — that's the real next step now.",

                        "input": {
                            "description": "Full brain-inspired architecture: encoder, working memory, surprise gate, fast memory, periodic consolidation into long-term store, and modular (MoE-style) routing",
                            "file_text": "\"\"\"\nFULL brain-inspired continual-learning prototype.\nLayers implemented:\n  0 - Encoder (slow weights, per-expert)\n  1 - Working memory buffer (temporary, holds recent (h, loss) pairs)\n  2 - Surprise gate (per-sample loss = salience signal)\n  3 - Fast memory (per-expert, decays if unreinforced, updates via local rule)\n  4 - Consolidation (periodic: promotes reinforced/high-salience buffer items\n                     into long-term store, then clears buffer)\n  5 - Long-term store (external key-value memory, retrieved by similarity ->\n                     RAG-style; NOT a slow-weight fine-tune in this version,\n                     see notes at bottom for why)\n  6 - Modular routing (MoE-style: router picks which expert's encoder+memory\n                     handles this input, so unrelated content doesn't share\n                     the same fast-memory slot)\n\nRun:  pip install torch numpy   then   python full_model.py\n\"\"\"\n\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\nimport numpy as np\n\ntorch.manual_seed(42)\nrng = np.random.default_rng(42)\n\n# =================================================================\n# 0. Toy continual-learning task (Task A, Task B; same space, diff clusters)\n# =================================================================\nN_FEATURES = 10\nN_CLASSES = 3\n\ndef make_task(n_samples, centers, noise=0.5):\n    X, y = [], []\n    for c, center in enumerate(centers):\n        pts = center + rng.normal(0, noise, size=(n_samples, N_FEATURES))\n        X.append(pts); y.append(np.full(n_samples, c))\n    X = np.concatenate(X); y = np.concatenate(y)\n    idx = rng.permutation(len(X))\n    return torch.tensor(X[idx], dtype=torch.float32), torch.tensor(y[idx], dtype=torch.long)\n\ncenters_A = rng.normal(0, 3, size=(N_CLASSES, N_FEATURES))\ncenters_B = rng.normal(0, 3, size=(N_CLASSES, N_FEATURES))\nX_A, y_A = make_task(300, centers_A)\nX_B, y_B = make_task(300, centers_B)\nX_A_test, y_A_test = make_task(100, centers_A)\nX_B_test, y_B_test = make_task(100, centers_B)\n\n\n# =================================================================\n# Layer 5 - Long-term store: simple external key-value memory (RAG-style).\n# Keys = encoder outputs at time of consolidation, Values = class label\n# (stand-in for \"compressed gist\"). Retrieval = nearest-neighbor similarity.\n# =================================================================\nclass LongTermStore:\n    def __init__(self, dim, capacity=200):\n        self.dim = dim\n        self.capacity = capacity\n        self.keys = torch.zeros(0, dim)\n        self.values = torch.zeros(0, dtype=torch.long)\n        self.strength = torch.zeros(0)  # how many times reinforced (repetition lever)\n\n    def write(self, key, value):\n        key = key.detach().unsqueeze(0)\n        # if very similar key already stored, reinforce it instead of duplicating\n        if len(self.keys) > 0:\n            sims = F.cosine_similarity(self.keys, key.expand(len(self.keys), -1))\n            best = sims.argmax().item()\n            if sims[best] > 0.95:\n                self.strength[best] += 1\n                return\n        self.keys = torch.cat([self.keys, key], dim=0)\n        self.values = torch.cat([self.values, torch.tensor([value])])\n        self.strength = torch.cat([self.strength, torch.tensor([1.0])])\n        # capacity limit: drop weakest entry if over capacity (forced forgetting)\n        if len(self.keys) > self.capacity:\n            weakest = self.strength.argmin().item()\n            keep = [i for i in range(len(self.keys)) if i != weakest]\n            self.keys, self.values, self.strength = self.keys[keep], self.values[keep], self.strength[keep]\n\n    def retrieve(self, query, k=1):\n        if len(self.keys) == 0:\n            return None\n        sims = F.cosine_similarity(self.keys, query.unsqueeze(0).expand(len(self.keys), -1))\n        topk = sims.topk(min(k, len(sims))).indices\n        return self.values[topk], sims[topk]\n\n    def __len__(self):\n        return len(self.keys)\n\n\n# =================================================================\n# One \"expert\": Layer 0 encoder + Layer 3 fast memory, for ONE region/module\n# =================================================================\nclass Expert(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, in_dim, hidden_dim, n_classes, decay=0.05):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.encoder = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(in_dim, hidden_dim), nn.ReLU())\n        self.classifier = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, n_classes)\n        self.memory = torch.zeros(hidden_dim)   # fast memory, NOT an nn.Parameter\n        self.decay = decay\n\n    def forward(self, x):\n        h = self.encoder(x)\n        h_with_mem = h + self.memory.unsqueeze(0)\n        logits = self.classifier(h_with_mem)\n        return logits, h\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def update_fast_memory(self, h_batch, per_sample_loss, surprise_threshold):\n        surprise = per_sample_loss.mean().item()\n        if surprise > surprise_threshold:\n            w = (per_sample_loss / (per_sample_loss.sum() + 1e-8)).unsqueeze(1)\n            update = (w * h_batch.detach()).sum(dim=0)\n            self.memory = (1 - self.decay) * self.memory + self.decay * update\n        else:\n            self.memory = (1 - self.decay) * self.memory\n        return surprise\n\n\n# =================================================================\n# Layer 6 - Router: decides which expert handles a given input\n# (simple version: a small learned classifier over input space itself)\n# =================================================================\nclass Router(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, in_dim, n_experts):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.net = nn.Linear(in_dim, n_experts)\n\n    def forward(self, x):\n        logits = self.net(x)\n        return logits.argmax(dim=1), F.softmax(logits, dim=1)\n\n\n# =================================================================\n# Full model: Router -> Expert(s) -> fast memory -> working buffer ->\n#             periodic consolidation -> long-term store\n# =================================================================\nclass FullBrainInspiredModel(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, in_dim, hidden_dim, n_classes, n_experts=2,\n                 decay=0.05, surprise_threshold=0.8,\n                 buffer_size=64, consolidate_every=50, repetition_min=2):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.router = Router(in_dim, n_experts)\n        self.experts = nn.ModuleList([\n            Expert(in_dim, hidden_dim, n_classes, decay=decay) for _ in range(n_experts)\n        ])\n        self.n_experts = n_experts\n        self.surprise_threshold = surprise_threshold\n\n        # Layer 1: working memory buffer (per expert): list of (h, loss, label)\n        self.buffers = [[] for _ in range(n_experts)]\n        self.buffer_size = buffer_size\n\n        # Layer 5: one long-term store per expert (keeps unrelated content separate)\n        self.long_term = [LongTermStore(hidden_dim) for _ in range(n_experts)]\n\n        self.step_count = 0\n        self.consolidate_every = consolidate_every\n        self.repetition_min = repetition_min  # min times seen before consolidation eligible\n\n        # logging (obstacle #3: log every internal signal separately)\n        self.log = {\"surprise\": [], \"memory_norm\": [[] for _ in range(n_experts)],\n                    \"expert_usage\": [], \"long_term_size\": [[] for _ in range(n_experts)]}\n\n    def forward(self, x, y=None, train=True):\n        expert_ids, route_probs = self.router(x)\n        self.log[\"expert_usage\"].append(expert_ids.float().mean().item())\n\n        all_logits = torch.zeros(x.size(0), self.experts[0].classifier.out_features)\n        all_h = torch.zeros(x.size(0), self.experts[0].encoder[0].out_features)\n\n        for e_id in range(self.n_experts):\n            mask = expert_ids == e_id\n            if mask.sum() == 0:\n                continue\n            logits_e, h_e = self.experts[e_id](x[mask])\n            all_logits[mask] = logits_e\n            all_h[mask] = h_e\n\n            if train and y is not None:\n                loss_e = F.cross_entropy(logits_e, y[mask], reduction=\"none\")\n                surprise = self.experts[e_id].update_fast_memory(h_e, loss_e, self.surprise_threshold)\n                self.log[\"surprise\"].append(surprise)\n                self.log[\"memory_norm\"][e_id].append(self.experts[e_id].memory.norm().item())\n\n                # Layer 1: push into working buffer\n                for i in range(len(h_e)):\n                    self.buffers[e_id].append((h_e[i].detach().clone(), loss_e[i].item(), y[mask][i].item()))\n                if len(self.buffers[e_id]) > self.buffer_size:\n                    self.buffers[e_id] = self.buffers[e_id][-self.buffer_size:]\n\n        if train:\n            self.step_count += 1\n            if self.step_count % self.consolidate_every == 0:\n                self.consolidate()\n\n        return all_logits, all_h, expert_ids\n\n    def consolidate(self):\n        \"\"\"Layer 4: periodic, sleep-like pass. Promote buffer items that were\n        either (a) seen/reinforced repeatedly (similar key appears >= repetition_min\n        times), or (b) had high salience (loss above threshold), into long-term store.\"\"\"\n        for e_id in range(self.n_experts):\n            buf = self.buffers[e_id]\n            if not buf:\n                continue\n            for h, loss, label in buf:\n                if loss > self.surprise_threshold or self.repetition_min <= 1:\n                    self.long_term[e_id].write(h, label)\n            self.log[\"long_term_size\"][e_id].append(len(self.long_term[e_id]))\n            self.buffers[e_id] = []  # clear buffer after consolidation (like sleep clearing hippocampus)\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def predict_with_long_term_fallback(self, x):\n        \"\"\"At inference: use expert's fast path, but also check long-term store\n        for a confident nearest-neighbor match (mimics recognition/familiarity retrieval).\"\"\"\n        expert_ids, _ = self.router(x)\n        preds = torch.zeros(x.size(0), dtype=torch.long)\n        for i in range(x.size(0)):\n            e_id = expert_ids[i].item()\n            logits, h = self.experts[e_id](x[i:i+1])\n            model_pred = logits.argmax(dim=1).item()\n\n            retrieved = self.long_term[e_id].retrieve(h.squeeze(0), k=1)\n            if retrieved is not None:\n                values, sims = retrieved\n                if sims[0].item() > 0.9:   # confident familiarity match\n                    preds[i] = values[0].item()\n                    continue\n            preds[i] = model_pred\n        return preds\n\n\n# =================================================================\n# Training / evaluation\n# =================================================================\ndef evaluate(model, X, y):\n    model.eval()\n    with torch.no_grad():\n        preds = model.predict_with_long_term_fallback(X)\n        acc = (preds == y).float().mean().item()\n    model.train()\n    return acc\n\ndef train_on_task(model, optimizer, X, y, epochs=30, batch_size=32):\n    n = len(X)\n    for epoch in range(epochs):\n        idx = torch.randperm(n)\n        for i in range(0, n, batch_size):\n            b = idx[i:i+batch_size]\n            xb, yb = X[b], y[b]\n            optimizer.zero_grad()\n            logits, h, expert_ids = model(xb, yb, train=True)\n            loss = F.cross_entropy(logits, yb)\n            loss.backward()\n            optimizer.step()\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    model = FullBrainInspiredModel(\n        in_dim=N_FEATURES, hidden_dim=32, n_classes=N_CLASSES, n_experts=2,\n        decay=0.05, surprise_threshold=0.8, buffer_size=64,\n        consolidate_every=20, repetition_min=1,\n    )\n    optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.01)\n\n    print(\"=== Training on Task A ===\")\n    train_on_task(model, optimizer, X_A, y_A, epochs=30)\n    model.consolidate()  # final consolidation pass after task\n    acc_A_after_A = evaluate(model, X_A_test, y_A_test)\n    print(f\"Accuracy on Task A right after learning A: {acc_A_after_A:.3f}\")\n    print(f\"Long-term store sizes per expert: {[len(s) for s in model.long_term]}\")\n\n    print(\"\\n=== Training on Task B (Task A no longer seen) ===\")\n    train_on_task(model, optimizer, X_B, y_B, epochs=30)\n    model.consolidate()\n    acc_A_after_B = evaluate(model, X_A_test, y_A_test)\n    acc_B_after_B = evaluate(model, X_B_test, y_B_test)\n\n    print(f\"Accuracy on Task A AFTER learning B: {acc_A_after_B:.3f}  <- check forgetting here\")\n    print(f\"Accuracy on Task B after learning B: {acc_B_after_B:.3f}\")\n    print(f\"Long-term store sizes per expert: {[len(s) for s in model.long_term]}\")\n    print(f\"Expert usage (mean id over time, first vs last 10 batches): \"\n          f\"{np.mean(model.log['expert_usage'][:10]):.2f} -> {np.mean(model.log['expert_usage'][-10:]):.2f}\")\n    print(f\"Final fast-memory norms per expert: {[e.memory.norm().item() for e in model.experts]}\")\n\n\"\"\"\nNOTES / HONEST LIMITATIONS OF THIS PROTOTYPE:\n- Layer 5 uses an EXTERNAL key-value store (RAG-style), not a slow-weight\n  fine-tune, because that path is simpler to get working first and matches\n  option (b) from our design discussion. Option (a) (folding consolidated\n  memories back into encoder weights via periodic fine-tuning) is a valid\n  next experiment -- swap consolidate() to also run a few backprop steps\n  on stored (h, label) pairs.\n- The Router is untrained-from-scratch and may route inconsistently early\n  on; consider pretraining it or using a fixed rule (e.g. task ID) first\n  to isolate whether memory/consolidation help BEFORE also debugging routing.\n- surprise_threshold, decay, consolidate_every, repetition_min are all\n  UNTUNED guesses. Expect to need real experimentation here (obstacle #2\n  and #5 from our earlier list) before results are meaningful.\n- This is still a small toy testbed, not a claim of solving continual\n  learning -- it is the \"first testable slice\" made complete across all\n  six layers we designed, so you can now run real ablations (turn off\n  routing, turn off long-term store, etc.) and see what each piece adds.\n\"\"\"\n",

Promote buffer items that were\\n        either (a) seen/reinforced repeatedly (similar key appears >= repetition_min\\n        times), or (b) had high salience (loss above threshold), into long-term store.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        for e_id in range(self.n_experts):\\n            buf = self.buffers[e_id]\\n            if not buf:\\n                continue\\n            for h, loss, label in buf:\\n                if loss > self.surprise_threshold or self.repetition_min <= 1:\\n                    self.long_term[e_id].write(h, label)\\n            self.log[\\\"long_term_size\\\"][e_id].append(len(self.long_term[e_id]))\\n            self.buffers[e_id] = []  # clear buffer after consolidation (like sleep clearing hippocampus)\\n\\n    @torch.no_grad()\\n    def predict_with_long_term_fallback(self, x):\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"At inference: use expert's fast path, but also check long-term store\\n        for a confident nearest-neighbor match (mimics recognition/familiarity retrieval).\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        expert_ids, _ = self.router(x)\\n        preds = torch.zeros(x.size(0), dtype=torch.long)\\n        for i in range(x.size(0)):\\n            e_id = expert_ids[i].item()\\n            logits, h = self.experts[e_id](x[i:i+1])\\n            model_pred = logits.argmax(dim=1).item()\\n\\n            retrieved = self.long_term[e_id].retrieve(h.squeeze(0), k=1)\\n            if retrieved is not None:\\n                values, sims = retrieved\\n                if sims[0].item() > 0.9:   # confident familiarity match\\n                    preds[i] = values[0].item()\\n                    continue\\n            preds[i] = model_pred\\n        return preds\\n\\n\\n# =================================================================\\n# Training / evaluation\\n# =================================================================\\ndef evaluate(model, X, y):\\n    model.eval()\\n    with torch.no_grad():\\n        preds = model.predict_with_long_term_fallback(X)\\n        acc = (preds == y).float().mean().item()\\n    model.train()\\n    return acc\\n\\ndef train_on_task(model, optimizer, X, y, epochs=30, batch_size=32):\\n    n = len(X)\\n    for epoch in range(epochs):\\n        idx = torch.randperm(n)\\n        for i in range(0, n, batch_size):\\n            b = idx[i:i+batch_size]\\n            xb, yb = X[b], y[b]\\n            optimizer.zero_grad()\\n            logits, h, expert_ids = model(xb, yb, train=True)\\n            loss = F.cross_entropy(logits, yb)\\n            loss.backward()\\n            optimizer.step()\\n\\n\\nif __name__ == \\\"__main__\\\":\\n    model = FullBrainInspiredModel(\\n        in_dim=N_FEATURES, hidden_dim=32, n_classes=N_CLASSES, n_experts=2,\\n        decay=0.05, surprise_threshold=0.8, buffer_size=64,\\n        consolidate_every=20, repetition_min=1,\\n    )\\n    optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.01)\\n\\n    print(\\\"=== Training on Task A ===\\\")\\n    train_on_task(model, optimizer, X_A, y_A, epochs=30)\\n    model.consolidate()  # final consolidation pass after task\\n    acc_A_after_A = evaluate(model, X_A_test, y_A_test)\\n    print(f\\\"Accuracy on Task A right after learning A: {acc_A_after_A:.3f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"Long-term store sizes per expert: {[len(s) for s in model.long_term]}\\\")\\n\\n    print(\\\"\\\\n=== Training on Task B (Task A no longer seen) ===\\\")\\n    train_on_task(model, optimizer, X_B, y_B, epochs=30)\\n    model.consolidate()\\n    acc_A_after_B = evaluate(model, X_A_test, y_A_test)\\n    acc_B_after_B = evaluate(model, X_B_test, y_B_test)\\n\\n    print(f\\\"Accuracy on Task A AFTER learning B: {acc_A_after_B:.3f}  <- check forgetting here\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"Accuracy on Task B after learning B: {acc_B_after_B:.3f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"Long-term store sizes per expert: {[len(s) for s in model.long_term]}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"Expert usage (mean id over time, first vs last 10 batches): \\\"\\n          f\\\"{np.mean(model.log['expert_usage'][:10]):.2f} -> {np.mean(model.log['expert_usage'][-10:]):.2f}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"Final fast-memory norms per expert: {[e.memory.norm().item() for e in model.experts]}\\\")\\n\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\nNOTES / HONEST LIMITATIONS OF THIS PROTOTYPE:\\n- Layer 5 uses an EXTERNAL key-value store (RAG-style), not a slow-weight\\n  fine-tune, because that path is simpler to get working first and matches\\n  option (b) from our design discussion. Option (a) (folding consolidated\\n  memories back into encoder weights via periodic fine-tuning) is a valid\\n  next experiment -- swap consolidate() to also run a few backprop steps\\n  on stored (h, label) pairs.\\n- The Router is untrained-from-scratch and may route inconsistently early\\n  on; consider pretraining it or using a fixed rule (e.g. task ID) first\\n  to isolate whether memory/consolidation help BEFORE also debugging routing.\\n- surprise_threshold, decay, consolidate_every, repetition_min are all\\n  UNTUNED guesses. Expect to need real experimentation here (obstacle #2\\n  and #5 from our earlier list) before results are meaningful.\\n- This is still a small toy testbed, not a claim of solving continual\\n  learning -- it is the \\\"first testable slice\\\" made complete across all\\n  six layers we designed, so you can now run real ablations (turn off\\n  routing, turn off long-term store, etc.) and see what each piece adds.\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\", \"filename\": \"/mnt/user-data/outputs/full_model.py\"}"
